From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to get past these thoughts that something terrible could happen if I miss something. I work with systems and access and settings, so maybe that’s part of how my mind frames it, but it’s not just normal caution. It’s like if there’s one access rule I didn’t check, one setting that isn’t right, one warning sign I overlooked, then someone could get hurt or lose everything, and somehow it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I know, at least part of me knows, that it doesn’t always make sense or that I’m taking on too much responsibility. I can say that logically. But when the thought hits, it feels very real, like I’m personally responsible right then and I can’t just move on from it. It kind of takes over. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and alleviate these thoughts and the anxiety they bring?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not like a little double-check and then I’m done. I get stuck in loops.\n\nAt work, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, even if I wrote them myself and already reviewed them. I’ll go back through audit logs again and again, looking for some sign that I missed something. Sometimes it’s old messages too — like emails or chats — where I’m trying to confirm what someone said or whether I warned them properly or whether there was some detail I should have acted on. And it doesn’t really matter that I just read it two minutes earlier. It feels like it didn’t “land” right, if that makes sense, so I have to do it again.\n\nThere’s also this counting thing I do in my head. I’ll mentally go through steps in a sequence — check this, verify that, confirm the lock, confirm the setting — and I keep doing it until it feels safe or complete. Not safe in a rational way, exactly. More like there’s a certain feeling I’m waiting for, and if I stop before I get it, I feel this spike of panic.\n\nAnd it’s not just computer systems. I’ll check door locks too, like at the office, and then I’ll walk away and immediately doubt whether I really checked. So I go back. Then I question whether I checked it carefully enough. It’s exhausting and embarrassing, honestly.\n\nThere have been nights where everyone else has gone home and I’ve stayed for hours, just rereading plans or logs or checking things because I couldn’t make myself leave. I know I’m probably not finding anything new by the tenth time, but stopping before it feels complete makes me feel like I’m taking a terrible risk.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and rituals interfering with your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten to the point where it’s not just “I’m stressed at work” anymore. It’s actually changing how people deal with me.\n\nThe biggest example is that I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions. I kept needing one more review, one more confirmation, one more pass through the risks, and then when I’d get close to approving something, I’d start doubting whether I had really understood the implications. So instead of moving things forward, I basically became the bottleneck. And that’s hard to say, because I’m supposed to be the person who helps make those calls.\n\nMy team has started working around me now. They’re not rude about it, but I can tell. They’ll make smaller decisions without looping me in, or they’ll route things through someone else because they know if it comes to me, it may get stuck. And part of me understands why they’re doing it, but it feels awful. I used to be someone people relied on, and now I feel like I’m this obstacle they have to manage.\n\nOutside of work, it’s affected things too. I used to play basketball pretty regularly, and I went to salsa nights — those were sort of the things that kept me normal, I guess. They got me out of my head and around people. But I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just too busy or I needed to finish something, but then weeks go by, and people stop asking as much. So I’m losing those routines and those people, and then I’m just more alone with all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these rituals, such as rechecking or counting, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like in the moment. I know it probably sounds extreme, but when I’m caught in it, rereading the deployment plan one more time or going back through the audit logs doesn’t feel optional. It feels like if I don’t do it, I’m leaving some dangerous thing unchecked.\n\nAnd it’s not even always that I find something. Most of the time I don’t. But my mind says, “What if you missed it because you didn’t read carefully enough?” or “What if the one line you skimmed is the one that matters?” So I read it again. Then sometimes again. Same with old messages — I’ll look back to see whether someone confirmed something, or whether I warned them clearly enough, and even after I see it, I still don’t feel settled.\n\nThe counting is similar. I’ll go through steps in my head in order, like I’m trying to make sure the sequence is complete. Check this, verify that, confirm the setting, confirm the lock. If I stop before it feels “safe” or finished, I get this immediate panic, like I’m being reckless.\n\nAnd with door locks, I can physically check them and still walk away thinking, “Did I really check?” Then I go back. It’s embarrassing, but yes, it feels necessary. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t make myself stop before it felt complete. It’s like leaving without doing it would be the same as allowing something bad to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these terrible things you're worried about could actually happen if you don't perform these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part that scares me, because if you ask me sitting here, calmly, do I think one missed setting automatically means disaster? I can say, no, not automatically. I understand that there are systems and safeguards and other people involved. I’m not completely unaware of that.\n\nBut when the thought hits, it feels very real. It’s like, if I miss one access rule, or one configuration setting, or one warning sign that I should have caught, then someone could get hurt, or someone could lose everything, or something could go badly wrong and it would be because I didn’t do the thing I was supposed to do. It lands on me as personal responsibility, not just a normal mistake.\n\nSo yes, part of me believes it could actually happen. Or maybe it’s more like I can’t prove that it won’t happen, and that uncertainty becomes unbearable. Even if another part of me knows I’m being irrational or taking it too far, the feeling is so strong that I can’t just say, “Well, that’s unlikely,” and move on. It feels like moving on would be irresponsible. Like I’d be choosing to ignore a danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop these thoughts from coming into your mind when they start?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I can sometimes distract myself for a minute, or I can tell myself, “This is probably not rational, you’ve already thought this through,” but that doesn’t stop it from showing up.\n\nIt’s more like the thought forces its way in. I’ll be trying to do something normal, and suddenly it’s there: what if I missed one setting, one access rule, one warning sign? What if that one thing is the thing that causes someone to get hurt, or lose everything, or have their life seriously affected? And then it’s not just a passing worry. It hits me like it’s my responsibility personally, like if I don’t catch it, then I allowed it to happen.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t seem to shut off. Even when another part of me knows, logically, that I’m taking it too far, the thought doesn’t feel like something I can just dismiss. It keeps pushing back into my mind, and the more I try to move on, the more it feels like I’m being careless or ignoring something dangerous. So no, I don’t feel like I can stop them once they start. At best I can argue with them, but they don’t really go away just because I want them to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are something you can control or are they completely outside of your control?\n\nPatient: They feel mostly outside of my control. Not completely in the sense that I can sometimes try to redirect myself or talk back to them, but I don’t feel like I choose them. I’m not sitting there deciding, “Let me worry about access rules again,” or “Let me imagine the worst-case scenario.” It just arrives, and once it does, it takes over.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that pattern — I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows, that sounds irrational or at least exaggerated. I can explain to myself that one person can’t control every possible outcome, that there are layers and reviews and other people involved. But emotionally it doesn’t land that way. It hits me like I am personally responsible, like if I don’t catch the thing, then I’m the reason something terrible happens.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I have some control over how I respond after the thought is already there, but I don’t have much control over the thought coming in, and I don’t have much control over how urgent it feels. I can’t just say, “That’s unreasonable,” and move on like I wish I could. It keeps pulling me back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to change or avoid because of these thoughts and rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. The biggest thing is work. I’m a computer and information systems manager, so a lot of my job is making decisions, signing off on configurations, access, security, migration plans — things where there is always some risk, but you still have to make the best decision and move forward.\n\nAnd I… I couldn’t. We had a major cloud migration, and I missed the deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions. I kept circling back, checking, rechecking, asking myself if I had missed something, if one setting was wrong, if some permission was too broad or too narrow, if some warning sign was there and I didn’t see it. At some point it stopped being diligence and turned into paralysis. But in the moment it didn’t feel like paralysis, it felt like I was preventing a disaster.\n\nThe really painful part is that my team has started working around me. They don’t say it cruelly, but I can tell. Decisions get routed through someone else, or people prepare things in a way that doesn’t require me to be the final person, because relying on me slows everything down. And I hate saying that out loud, because I used to be the person people counted on when things were complicated. Now I feel like I’m becoming the bottleneck everyone has to manage.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve also stopped doing things I used to rely on. I used to play basketball, and I had salsa nights I’d go to, and those were not just hobbies — they were my routines, my people, the things that got me out of my head. But I’ve stopped showing up. Sometimes because I’m still stuck at work trying to get comfortable with a decision, sometimes because I’m mentally exhausted, and sometimes because I feel like I shouldn’t go enjoy myself if there’s still something unresolved that could matter.\n\nSo yes, I’ve changed my day-to-day life. It’s not just that I’m anxious in the background. It’s affecting deadlines, my role at work, my relationships with my team, and even the social routines that used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions, like touching or tapping, as part of your rituals?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. It’s not like I have to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or tap something with my fingers. For me it’s more… checking actions. Mental and work-related checking, mostly, although sometimes it becomes physical in the sense that I’ll go back and look at things again and again.\n\nLike I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, even after I know I’ve already read them. I’ll go back through audit logs, sometimes line by line, looking for anything that feels off. I’ll check door locks too, and that one embarrasses me because it feels so basic, but I’ll leave and then come back and check again because I can’t get comfortable with the idea that I really verified it.\n\nAnd old messages — emails, chat threads, notes — I’ll reread those too, trying to make sure I didn’t miss some warning or misunderstand what someone asked me to approve. Sometimes I’m not even learning anything new. I’m just trying to get that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe. But it doesn’t always come.\n\nI also count through steps in my head. Not always out loud, but internally — like, did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the access rule, did I review the logs, did I compare the plan against the requirement. I’ll run through it in a sequence until it feels complete or “safe,” I guess. And if I stop before it feels complete, I get this spike of panic, like I’m walking away from something dangerous.\n\nThere have been nights where I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t stop in the middle of that process. I would tell myself, just one more pass, just one more review, and then I’ll go. But then that pass would create another question, or it wouldn’t feel settled enough, so I’d start over. So no, not touching or tapping specifically — but yes, I do feel compelled to do these checking and rereading things, and it can be very hard to stop once I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself when you can't complete one of your checking rituals or when you try to stop thinking about these obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly.\n\nIf I try to stop before I’ve checked enough, or if I tell myself, “No, you’re not going back through that again,” it doesn’t feel like I’m being reasonable or efficient. It feels like I’m being careless. Like I’m choosing comfort over responsibility. And then this guilt comes in almost immediately — like, what if this is the one time there really is a setting I missed, or an access rule I misunderstood, or some warning sign that was right there and I just decided not to look because I was tired?\n\nI know how that sounds. Part of me can step back and say, “You can’t personally prevent every possible bad outcome. You can’t keep reviewing forever.” But that part is quiet compared to the feeling. The feeling is that if someone gets hurt, or if something goes wrong and people lose something important, then it’s on me because I had the thought and didn’t follow it all the way through.\n\nAnd even when I try to just move on, the guilt doesn’t really let me. It keeps arguing with me. It’s like, “You knew there might be a risk and you walked away anyway.” So yes, I blame myself, even when I understand logically that the level of responsibility I’m putting on myself doesn’t make sense. Emotionally it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are prevented from performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just discomfort, it’s more like this sudden panic that rises up when I’m forced to stop before the checking feels finished.\n\nLike if I’m rereading a deployment plan or going through audit logs and someone says, “We need to move on,” or I have to leave because it’s late, my mind doesn’t treat that as just stopping a task. It treats it like I’m walking away from something unsafe. I’ll feel this pressure in my chest and this urgent need to go back and check one more thing, then one more thing after that.\n\nIt happens with smaller things too, like door locks or old messages. I can know I checked them, but if I’m prevented from checking again before it feels “safe,” I get really agitated. I start counting through the steps in my head — did I verify this, did I confirm that, did I miss something — almost like I’m trying to recreate the checking mentally if I can’t do it physically.\n\nAnd at work it can get bad. There have been nights where everyone else has left and I’ve stayed for hours because stopping in the middle of that process felt unbearable. I’d tell myself I couldn’t leave until it felt complete. If I tried to leave before that, I’d get this spike of panic, like I was leaving behind some danger I should have caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels aimed at, most of the time. It’s not checking for the sake of checking, or because I enjoy being thorough. It feels like if I don’t do it, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to catch it.\n\nAt work, especially, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, and go back through audit logs again and again, because I’m afraid I missed something that could hurt people somehow — maybe not physically, but cause damage, expose data, break something important, affect customers, affect the company. I’ll tell myself I’m being responsible, but it goes way past normal responsibility. I’ll keep counting through the steps in my head — did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the rule — until it feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t feel safe, I can’t let it go.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. I do it with door locks too, where I know the fear is more basic, like someone could get in or something could happen because I didn’t make sure. Even with old messages, I’ll reread them because I worry there was some warning or instruction I missed, and if I move on without catching it, then whatever happens is partly my fault.\n\nSo yes, the checking feels like it’s supposed to prevent harm or prevent some serious mistake. The problem is that the amount of checking that would make me feel certain keeps moving. I can check once, twice, ten times, and still feel like maybe I missed something. That’s why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves — because stopping before it feels complete makes me panic, like I’m knowingly leaving danger behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to follow certain rules or a specific methodology when performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not always a formal written checklist, but it feels like there’s a very specific order I have to follow, or it doesn’t “count.”\n\nWith work things, especially deployment plans or audit logs, I’ll go through them in a certain sequence — like first I have to reread the plan, then check the logs, then mentally count through the steps I think I verified. And if I lose my place, or if someone interrupts me, or if I suddenly think, “Wait, did I really confirm that part?” then I’ll often feel like I have to go back and start parts of it again. Not because I want to be extra careful in a normal way, but because it doesn’t feel safe unless I’ve followed the process correctly.\n\nIt happens with door locks too. I can’t just glance and move on. I’ll check, then kind of mentally mark it off, and sometimes count through what I did in my head. And old messages — I’ll reread them in this same repetitive way, like I’m looking for some phrase or warning I might have missed.\n\nThe frustrating thing is that the rule isn’t always logical. It’s more like a feeling of completeness. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, once you’ve gone through these steps, you can stop,” but then it doesn’t feel complete, so I keep going. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves because I couldn’t get that feeling that the checking was finished. If I try to stop before it feels safe, I panic, like I’ve left something unresolved that could come back badly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll check door locks too, and that one embarrasses me because it feels so basic, but I’ll leave and then come back and check again because I can’t get comfortable with the idea that I really verified it.\n\nAnd old messages — emails, chat threads, notes — I’ll reread those too, trying to make sure I didn’t miss some warning or misunderstand what someone asked me to approve. Sometimes I’m not even learning anything new. I’m just trying to get that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe. But it doesn’t always come.\n\nI also count through steps in my head. Not always out loud, but internally — like, did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the access rule, did I review the logs, did I compare the plan against the requirement. I’ll run through it in a sequence until it feels complete or “safe,” I guess. And if I stop before it feels complete, I get this spike of panic, like I’m walking away from something dangerous.\n\nThere have been nights where I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t stop in the middle of that process. I would tell myself, just one more pass, just one more review, and then I’ll go. But then that pass would create another question, or it wouldn’t feel settled enough, so I’d start over. So no, not touching or tapping specifically — but yes, I do feel compelled to do these checking and rereading things, and it can be very hard to stop once I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself when you can't complete one of your checking rituals or when you try to stop thinking about these obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly.\n\nIf I try to stop before I’ve checked enough, or if I tell myself, “No, you’re not going back through that again,” it doesn’t feel like I’m being reasonable or efficient. It feels like I’m being careless. Like I’m choosing comfort over responsibility. And then this guilt comes in almost immediately — like, what if this is the one time there really is a setting I missed, or an access rule I misunderstood, or some warning sign that was right there and I just decided not to look because I was tired?\n\nI know how that sounds. Part of me can step back and say, “You can’t personally prevent every possible bad outcome. You can’t keep reviewing forever.” But that part is quiet compared to the feeling. The feeling is that if someone gets hurt, or if something goes wrong and people lose something important, then it’s on me because I had the thought and didn’t follow it all the way through.\n\nAnd even when I try to just move on, the guilt doesn’t really let me. It keeps arguing with me. It’s like, “You knew there might be a risk and you walked away anyway.” So yes, I blame myself, even when I understand logically that the level of responsibility I’m putting on myself doesn’t make sense. Emotionally it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are prevented from performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just discomfort, it’s more like this sudden panic that rises up when I’m forced to stop before the checking feels finished.\n\nLike if I’m rereading a deployment plan or going through audit logs and someone says, “We need to move on,” or I have to leave because it’s late, my mind doesn’t treat that as just stopping a task. It treats it like I’m walking away from something unsafe. I’ll feel this pressure in my chest and this urgent need to go back and check one more thing, then one more thing after that.\n\nIt happens with smaller things too, like door locks or old messages. I can know I checked them, but if I’m prevented from checking again before it feels “safe,” I get really agitated. I start counting through the steps in my head — did I verify this, did I confirm that, did I miss something — almost like I’m trying to recreate the checking mentally if I can’t do it physically.\n\nAnd at work it can get bad. There have been nights where everyone else has left and I’ve stayed for hours because stopping in the middle of that process felt unbearable. I’d tell myself I couldn’t leave until it felt complete. If I tried to leave before that, I’d get this spike of panic, like I was leaving behind some danger I should have caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels aimed at, most of the time. It’s not checking for the sake of checking, or because I enjoy being thorough. It feels like if I don’t do it, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to catch it.\n\nAt work, especially, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, and go back through audit logs again and again, because I’m afraid I missed something that could hurt people somehow — maybe not physically, but cause damage, expose data, break something important, affect customers, affect the company. I’ll tell myself I’m being responsible, but it goes way past normal responsibility. I’ll keep counting through the steps in my head — did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the rule — until it feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t feel safe, I can’t let it go.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. I do it with door locks too, where I know the fear is more basic, like someone could get in or something could happen because I didn’t make sure. Even with old messages, I’ll reread them because I worry there was some warning or instruction I missed, and if I move on without catching it, then whatever happens is partly my fault.\n\nSo yes, the checking feels like it’s supposed to prevent harm or prevent some serious mistake. The problem is that the amount of checking that would make me feel certain keeps moving. I can check once, twice, ten times, and still feel like maybe I missed something. That’s why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves — because stopping before it feels complete makes me panic, like I’m knowingly leaving danger behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to follow certain rules or a specific methodology when performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not always a formal written checklist, but it feels like there’s a very specific order I have to follow, or it doesn’t “count.”\n\nWith work things, especially deployment plans or audit logs, I’ll go through them in a certain sequence — like first I have to reread the plan, then check the logs, then mentally count through the steps I think I verified. And if I lose my place, or if someone interrupts me, or if I suddenly think, “Wait, did I really confirm that part?” then I’ll often feel like I have to go back and start parts of it again. Not because I want to be extra careful in a normal way, but because it doesn’t feel safe unless I’ve followed the process correctly.\n\nIt happens with door locks too. I can’t just glance and move on. I’ll check, then kind of mentally mark it off, and sometimes count through what I did in my head. And old messages — I’ll reread them in this same repetitive way, like I’m looking for some phrase or warning I might have missed.\n\nThe frustrating thing is that the rule isn’t always logical. It’s more like a feeling of completeness. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, once you’ve gone through these steps, you can stop,” but then it doesn’t feel complete, so I keep going. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves because I couldn’t get that feeling that the checking was finished. If I try to stop before it feels safe, I panic, like I’ve left something unresolved that could come back badly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much. I think that’s actually the part that scares me the most, because it’s not just something happening privately in my head anymore. It’s changing how I function, and how people treat me.

The biggest example is work. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I just could not finish signing off on decisions. I kept reviewing, revisiting, wanting one more confirmation, one more pass, one more assurance that we weren’t making the wrong call. And it wasn’t like I didn’t understand the technical issues — I did. That’s my job. But I couldn’t get to the point of saying, “Okay, this is good enough, we can move forward.” I got stuck in that loop, and the deadline passed.

And since then, I can tell my team has started working around me. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I notice it. Decisions get made without waiting for me the way they used to. People bring me things later, after they’ve already narrowed the options, or they try to route approvals through someone else because they know I might hold it up. That feels awful, honestly. I’m supposed to be the person they rely on, and now I feel like I’ve become a bottleneck they have to manage.

It’s affecting my personal life too. I used to have routines that kept me sane — basketball, salsa nights, just being around people and not being inside work or inside my own head all the time. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I’d tell myself I was just too busy, or I had something important to finish, but after a while I realized I was just disappearing from those parts of my life. I’m losing contact with people, and I’m losing the things that made me feel grounded.

So yes, it’s definitely interfering with my quality of life. It’s affecting my work, my reputation, my friendships, even the basic structure of my week. It feels like my life has gotten smaller around this need to be absolutely sure.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to get past these thoughts that something terrible could happen if I miss something. I work with systems and access and settings, so maybe that’s part of how my mind frames it, but it’s not just normal caution. It’s like if there’s one access rule I didn’t check, one setting that isn’t right, one warning sign I overlooked, then someone could get hurt or lose everything, and somehow it would be my fault.\n\nAnd I know, at least part of me knows, that it doesn’t always make sense or that I’m taking on too much responsibility. I can say that logically. But when the thought hits, it feels very real, like I’m personally responsible right then and I can’t just move on from it. It kind of takes over. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and alleviate these thoughts and the anxiety they bring?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not like a little double-check and then I’m done. I get stuck in loops.\n\nAt work, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, even if I wrote them myself and already reviewed them. I’ll go back through audit logs again and again, looking for some sign that I missed something. Sometimes it’s old messages too — like emails or chats — where I’m trying to confirm what someone said or whether I warned them properly or whether there was some detail I should have acted on. And it doesn’t really matter that I just read it two minutes earlier. It feels like it didn’t “land” right, if that makes sense, so I have to do it again.\n\nThere’s also this counting thing I do in my head. I’ll mentally go through steps in a sequence — check this, verify that, confirm the lock, confirm the setting — and I keep doing it until it feels safe or complete. Not safe in a rational way, exactly. More like there’s a certain feeling I’m waiting for, and if I stop before I get it, I feel this spike of panic.\n\nAnd it’s not just computer systems. I’ll check door locks too, like at the office, and then I’ll walk away and immediately doubt whether I really checked. So I go back. Then I question whether I checked it carefully enough. It’s exhausting and embarrassing, honestly.\n\nThere have been nights where everyone else has gone home and I’ve stayed for hours, just rereading plans or logs or checking things because I couldn’t make myself leave. I know I’m probably not finding anything new by the tenth time, but stopping before it feels complete makes me feel like I’m taking a terrible risk.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and rituals interfering with your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten to the point where it’s not just “I’m stressed at work” anymore. It’s actually changing how people deal with me.\n\nThe biggest example is that I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions. I kept needing one more review, one more confirmation, one more pass through the risks, and then when I’d get close to approving something, I’d start doubting whether I had really understood the implications. So instead of moving things forward, I basically became the bottleneck. And that’s hard to say, because I’m supposed to be the person who helps make those calls.\n\nMy team has started working around me now. They’re not rude about it, but I can tell. They’ll make smaller decisions without looping me in, or they’ll route things through someone else because they know if it comes to me, it may get stuck. And part of me understands why they’re doing it, but it feels awful. I used to be someone people relied on, and now I feel like I’m this obstacle they have to manage.\n\nOutside of work, it’s affected things too. I used to play basketball pretty regularly, and I went to salsa nights — those were sort of the things that kept me normal, I guess. They got me out of my head and around people. But I’ve stopped showing up. At first I told myself I was just too busy or I needed to finish something, but then weeks go by, and people stop asking as much. So I’m losing those routines and those people, and then I’m just more alone with all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these rituals, such as rechecking or counting, are necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like in the moment. I know it probably sounds extreme, but when I’m caught in it, rereading the deployment plan one more time or going back through the audit logs doesn’t feel optional. It feels like if I don’t do it, I’m leaving some dangerous thing unchecked.\n\nAnd it’s not even always that I find something. Most of the time I don’t. But my mind says, “What if you missed it because you didn’t read carefully enough?” or “What if the one line you skimmed is the one that matters?” So I read it again. Then sometimes again. Same with old messages — I’ll look back to see whether someone confirmed something, or whether I warned them clearly enough, and even after I see it, I still don’t feel settled.\n\nThe counting is similar. I’ll go through steps in my head in order, like I’m trying to make sure the sequence is complete. Check this, verify that, confirm the setting, confirm the lock. If I stop before it feels “safe” or finished, I get this immediate panic, like I’m being reckless.\n\nAnd with door locks, I can physically check them and still walk away thinking, “Did I really check?” Then I go back. It’s embarrassing, but yes, it feels necessary. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t make myself stop before it felt complete. It’s like leaving without doing it would be the same as allowing something bad to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these terrible things you're worried about could actually happen if you don't perform these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part that scares me, because if you ask me sitting here, calmly, do I think one missed setting automatically means disaster? I can say, no, not automatically. I understand that there are systems and safeguards and other people involved. I’m not completely unaware of that.\n\nBut when the thought hits, it feels very real. It’s like, if I miss one access rule, or one configuration setting, or one warning sign that I should have caught, then someone could get hurt, or someone could lose everything, or something could go badly wrong and it would be because I didn’t do the thing I was supposed to do. It lands on me as personal responsibility, not just a normal mistake.\n\nSo yes, part of me believes it could actually happen. Or maybe it’s more like I can’t prove that it won’t happen, and that uncertainty becomes unbearable. Even if another part of me knows I’m being irrational or taking it too far, the feeling is so strong that I can’t just say, “Well, that’s unlikely,” and move on. It feels like moving on would be irresponsible. Like I’d be choosing to ignore a danger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop these thoughts from coming into your mind when they start?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I can sometimes distract myself for a minute, or I can tell myself, “This is probably not rational, you’ve already thought this through,” but that doesn’t stop it from showing up.\n\nIt’s more like the thought forces its way in. I’ll be trying to do something normal, and suddenly it’s there: what if I missed one setting, one access rule, one warning sign? What if that one thing is the thing that causes someone to get hurt, or lose everything, or have their life seriously affected? And then it’s not just a passing worry. It hits me like it’s my responsibility personally, like if I don’t catch it, then I allowed it to happen.\n\nThat’s the part I can’t seem to shut off. Even when another part of me knows, logically, that I’m taking it too far, the thought doesn’t feel like something I can just dismiss. It keeps pushing back into my mind, and the more I try to move on, the more it feels like I’m being careless or ignoring something dangerous. So no, I don’t feel like I can stop them once they start. At best I can argue with them, but they don’t really go away just because I want them to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are something you can control or are they completely outside of your control?\n\nPatient: They feel mostly outside of my control. Not completely in the sense that I can sometimes try to redirect myself or talk back to them, but I don’t feel like I choose them. I’m not sitting there deciding, “Let me worry about access rules again,” or “Let me imagine the worst-case scenario.” It just arrives, and once it does, it takes over.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that pattern — I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows, that sounds irrational or at least exaggerated. I can explain to myself that one person can’t control every possible outcome, that there are layers and reviews and other people involved. But emotionally it doesn’t land that way. It hits me like I am personally responsible, like if I don’t catch the thing, then I’m the reason something terrible happens.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I have some control over how I respond after the thought is already there, but I don’t have much control over the thought coming in, and I don’t have much control over how urgent it feels. I can’t just say, “That’s unreasonable,” and move on like I wish I could. It keeps pulling me back.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you've had to change or avoid because of these thoughts and rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. The biggest thing is work. I’m a computer and information systems manager, so a lot of my job is making decisions, signing off on configurations, access, security, migration plans — things where there is always some risk, but you still have to make the best decision and move forward.\n\nAnd I… I couldn’t. We had a major cloud migration, and I missed the deadline because I couldn’t finish signing off on decisions. I kept circling back, checking, rechecking, asking myself if I had missed something, if one setting was wrong, if some permission was too broad or too narrow, if some warning sign was there and I didn’t see it. At some point it stopped being diligence and turned into paralysis. But in the moment it didn’t feel like paralysis, it felt like I was preventing a disaster.\n\nThe really painful part is that my team has started working around me. They don’t say it cruelly, but I can tell. Decisions get routed through someone else, or people prepare things in a way that doesn’t require me to be the final person, because relying on me slows everything down. And I hate saying that out loud, because I used to be the person people counted on when things were complicated. Now I feel like I’m becoming the bottleneck everyone has to manage.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve also stopped doing things I used to rely on. I used to play basketball, and I had salsa nights I’d go to, and those were not just hobbies — they were my routines, my people, the things that got me out of my head. But I’ve stopped showing up. Sometimes because I’m still stuck at work trying to get comfortable with a decision, sometimes because I’m mentally exhausted, and sometimes because I feel like I shouldn’t go enjoy myself if there’s still something unresolved that could matter.\n\nSo yes, I’ve changed my day-to-day life. It’s not just that I’m anxious in the background. It’s affecting deadlines, my role at work, my relationships with my team, and even the social routines that used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions, like touching or tapping, as part of your rituals?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. It’s not like I have to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or tap something with my fingers. For me it’s more… checking actions. Mental and work-related checking, mostly, although sometimes it becomes physical in the sense that I’ll go back and look at things again and again.\n\nLike I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, even after I know I’ve already read them. I’ll go back through audit logs, sometimes line by line, looking for anything that feels off. I’ll check door locks too, and that one embarrasses me because it feels so basic, but I’ll leave and then come back and check again because I can’t get comfortable with the idea that I really verified it.\n\nAnd old messages — emails, chat threads, notes — I’ll reread those too, trying to make sure I didn’t miss some warning or misunderstand what someone asked me to approve. Sometimes I’m not even learning anything new. I’m just trying to get that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe. But it doesn’t always come.\n\nI also count through steps in my head. Not always out loud, but internally — like, did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the access rule, did I review the logs, did I compare the plan against the requirement. I’ll run through it in a sequence until it feels complete or “safe,” I guess. And if I stop before it feels complete, I get this spike of panic, like I’m walking away from something dangerous.\n\nThere have been nights where I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t stop in the middle of that process. I would tell myself, just one more pass, just one more review, and then I’ll go. But then that pass would create another question, or it wouldn’t feel settled enough, so I’d start over. So no, not touching or tapping specifically — but yes, I do feel compelled to do these checking and rereading things, and it can be very hard to stop once I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself when you can't complete one of your checking rituals or when you try to stop thinking about these obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly.\n\nIf I try to stop before I’ve checked enough, or if I tell myself, “No, you’re not going back through that again,” it doesn’t feel like I’m being reasonable or efficient. It feels like I’m being careless. Like I’m choosing comfort over responsibility. And then this guilt comes in almost immediately — like, what if this is the one time there really is a setting I missed, or an access rule I misunderstood, or some warning sign that was right there and I just decided not to look because I was tired?\n\nI know how that sounds. Part of me can step back and say, “You can’t personally prevent every possible bad outcome. You can’t keep reviewing forever.” But that part is quiet compared to the feeling. The feeling is that if someone gets hurt, or if something goes wrong and people lose something important, then it’s on me because I had the thought and didn’t follow it all the way through.\n\nAnd even when I try to just move on, the guilt doesn’t really let me. It keeps arguing with me. It’s like, “You knew there might be a risk and you walked away anyway.” So yes, I blame myself, even when I understand logically that the level of responsibility I’m putting on myself doesn’t make sense. Emotionally it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are prevented from performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just discomfort, it’s more like this sudden panic that rises up when I’m forced to stop before the checking feels finished.\n\nLike if I’m rereading a deployment plan or going through audit logs and someone says, “We need to move on,” or I have to leave because it’s late, my mind doesn’t treat that as just stopping a task. It treats it like I’m walking away from something unsafe. I’ll feel this pressure in my chest and this urgent need to go back and check one more thing, then one more thing after that.\n\nIt happens with smaller things too, like door locks or old messages. I can know I checked them, but if I’m prevented from checking again before it feels “safe,” I get really agitated. I start counting through the steps in my head — did I verify this, did I confirm that, did I miss something — almost like I’m trying to recreate the checking mentally if I can’t do it physically.\n\nAnd at work it can get bad. There have been nights where everyone else has left and I’ve stayed for hours because stopping in the middle of that process felt unbearable. I’d tell myself I couldn’t leave until it felt complete. If I tried to leave before that, I’d get this spike of panic, like I was leaving behind some danger I should have caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels aimed at, most of the time. It’s not checking for the sake of checking, or because I enjoy being thorough. It feels like if I don’t do it, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to catch it.\n\nAt work, especially, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, and go back through audit logs again and again, because I’m afraid I missed something that could hurt people somehow — maybe not physically, but cause damage, expose data, break something important, affect customers, affect the company. I’ll tell myself I’m being responsible, but it goes way past normal responsibility. I’ll keep counting through the steps in my head — did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the rule — until it feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t feel safe, I can’t let it go.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. I do it with door locks too, where I know the fear is more basic, like someone could get in or something could happen because I didn’t make sure. Even with old messages, I’ll reread them because I worry there was some warning or instruction I missed, and if I move on without catching it, then whatever happens is partly my fault.\n\nSo yes, the checking feels like it’s supposed to prevent harm or prevent some serious mistake. The problem is that the amount of checking that would make me feel certain keeps moving. I can check once, twice, ten times, and still feel like maybe I missed something. That’s why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves — because stopping before it feels complete makes me panic, like I’m knowingly leaving danger behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to follow certain rules or a specific methodology when performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not always a formal written checklist, but it feels like there’s a very specific order I have to follow, or it doesn’t “count.”\n\nWith work things, especially deployment plans or audit logs, I’ll go through them in a certain sequence — like first I have to reread the plan, then check the logs, then mentally count through the steps I think I verified. And if I lose my place, or if someone interrupts me, or if I suddenly think, “Wait, did I really confirm that part?” then I’ll often feel like I have to go back and start parts of it again. Not because I want to be extra careful in a normal way, but because it doesn’t feel safe unless I’ve followed the process correctly.\n\nIt happens with door locks too. I can’t just glance and move on. I’ll check, then kind of mentally mark it off, and sometimes count through what I did in my head. And old messages — I’ll reread them in this same repetitive way, like I’m looking for some phrase or warning I might have missed.\n\nThe frustrating thing is that the rule isn’t always logical. It’s more like a feeling of completeness. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, once you’ve gone through these steps, you can stop,” but then it doesn’t feel complete, so I keep going. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves because I couldn’t get that feeling that the checking was finished. If I try to stop before it feels safe, I panic, like I’ve left something unresolved that could come back badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your obsessive thoughts and need to perform these rituals are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. I think that’s actually the part that scares me the most, because it’s not just something happening privately in my head anymore. It’s changing how I function, and how people treat me.\n\nThe biggest example is work. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I just could not finish signing off on decisions. I kept reviewing, revisiting, wanting one more confirmation, one more pass, one more assurance that we weren’t making the wrong call. And it wasn’t like I didn’t understand the technical issues — I did. That’s my job. But I couldn’t get to the point of saying, “Okay, this is good enough, we can move forward.” I got stuck in that loop, and the deadline passed.\n\nAnd since then, I can tell my team has started working around me. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I notice it. Decisions get made without waiting for me the way they used to. People bring me things later, after they’ve already narrowed the options, or they try to route approvals through someone else because they know I might hold it up. That feels awful, honestly. I’m supposed to be the person they rely on, and now I feel like I’ve become a bottleneck they have to manage.\n\nIt’s affecting my personal life too. I used to have routines that kept me sane — basketball, salsa nights, just being around people and not being inside work or inside my own head all the time. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I’d tell myself I was just too busy, or I had something important to finish, but after a while I realized I was just disappearing from those parts of my life. I’m losing contact with people, and I’m losing the things that made me feel grounded.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering with my quality of life. It’s affecting my work, my reputation, my friendships, even the basic structure of my week. It feels like my life has gotten smaller around this need to be absolutely sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes because I’m still stuck at work trying to get comfortable with a decision, sometimes because I’m mentally exhausted, and sometimes because I feel like I shouldn’t go enjoy myself if there’s still something unresolved that could matter.\n\nSo yes, I’ve changed my day-to-day life. It’s not just that I’m anxious in the background. It’s affecting deadlines, my role at work, my relationships with my team, and even the social routines that used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions, like touching or tapping, as part of your rituals?\n\nPatient: Not really touching or tapping, no. It’s not like I have to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or tap something with my fingers. For me it’s more… checking actions. Mental and work-related checking, mostly, although sometimes it becomes physical in the sense that I’ll go back and look at things again and again.\n\nLike I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, even after I know I’ve already read them. I’ll go back through audit logs, sometimes line by line, looking for anything that feels off. I’ll check door locks too, and that one embarrasses me because it feels so basic, but I’ll leave and then come back and check again because I can’t get comfortable with the idea that I really verified it.\n\nAnd old messages — emails, chat threads, notes — I’ll reread those too, trying to make sure I didn’t miss some warning or misunderstand what someone asked me to approve. Sometimes I’m not even learning anything new. I’m just trying to get that feeling of, okay, now it’s safe. But it doesn’t always come.\n\nI also count through steps in my head. Not always out loud, but internally — like, did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the access rule, did I review the logs, did I compare the plan against the requirement. I’ll run through it in a sequence until it feels complete or “safe,” I guess. And if I stop before it feels complete, I get this spike of panic, like I’m walking away from something dangerous.\n\nThere have been nights where I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else left because I couldn’t stop in the middle of that process. I would tell myself, just one more pass, just one more review, and then I’ll go. But then that pass would create another question, or it wouldn’t feel settled enough, so I’d start over. So no, not touching or tapping specifically — but yes, I do feel compelled to do these checking and rereading things, and it can be very hard to stop once I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself when you can't complete one of your checking rituals or when you try to stop thinking about these obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s probably one of the worst parts of it, honestly.\n\nIf I try to stop before I’ve checked enough, or if I tell myself, “No, you’re not going back through that again,” it doesn’t feel like I’m being reasonable or efficient. It feels like I’m being careless. Like I’m choosing comfort over responsibility. And then this guilt comes in almost immediately — like, what if this is the one time there really is a setting I missed, or an access rule I misunderstood, or some warning sign that was right there and I just decided not to look because I was tired?\n\nI know how that sounds. Part of me can step back and say, “You can’t personally prevent every possible bad outcome. You can’t keep reviewing forever.” But that part is quiet compared to the feeling. The feeling is that if someone gets hurt, or if something goes wrong and people lose something important, then it’s on me because I had the thought and didn’t follow it all the way through.\n\nAnd even when I try to just move on, the guilt doesn’t really let me. It keeps arguing with me. It’s like, “You knew there might be a risk and you walked away anyway.” So yes, I blame myself, even when I understand logically that the level of responsibility I’m putting on myself doesn’t make sense. Emotionally it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are prevented from performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very anxious. It’s not just discomfort, it’s more like this sudden panic that rises up when I’m forced to stop before the checking feels finished.\n\nLike if I’m rereading a deployment plan or going through audit logs and someone says, “We need to move on,” or I have to leave because it’s late, my mind doesn’t treat that as just stopping a task. It treats it like I’m walking away from something unsafe. I’ll feel this pressure in my chest and this urgent need to go back and check one more thing, then one more thing after that.\n\nIt happens with smaller things too, like door locks or old messages. I can know I checked them, but if I’m prevented from checking again before it feels “safe,” I get really agitated. I start counting through the steps in my head — did I verify this, did I confirm that, did I miss something — almost like I’m trying to recreate the checking mentally if I can’t do it physically.\n\nAnd at work it can get bad. There have been nights where everyone else has left and I’ve stayed for hours because stopping in the middle of that process felt unbearable. I’d tell myself I couldn’t leave until it felt complete. If I tried to leave before that, I’d get this spike of panic, like I was leaving behind some danger I should have caught.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking rituals are specifically aimed at preventing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels aimed at, most of the time. It’s not checking for the sake of checking, or because I enjoy being thorough. It feels like if I don’t do it, something bad could happen and it would be because I failed to catch it.\n\nAt work, especially, I’ll reread deployment plans over and over, and go back through audit logs again and again, because I’m afraid I missed something that could hurt people somehow — maybe not physically, but cause damage, expose data, break something important, affect customers, affect the company. I’ll tell myself I’m being responsible, but it goes way past normal responsibility. I’ll keep counting through the steps in my head — did I check this, did I verify that, did I confirm the rule — until it feels “safe,” and if it doesn’t feel safe, I can’t let it go.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. I do it with door locks too, where I know the fear is more basic, like someone could get in or something could happen because I didn’t make sure. Even with old messages, I’ll reread them because I worry there was some warning or instruction I missed, and if I move on without catching it, then whatever happens is partly my fault.\n\nSo yes, the checking feels like it’s supposed to prevent harm or prevent some serious mistake. The problem is that the amount of checking that would make me feel certain keeps moving. I can check once, twice, ten times, and still feel like maybe I missed something. That’s why I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves — because stopping before it feels complete makes me panic, like I’m knowingly leaving danger behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to follow certain rules or a specific methodology when performing your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not always a formal written checklist, but it feels like there’s a very specific order I have to follow, or it doesn’t “count.”\n\nWith work things, especially deployment plans or audit logs, I’ll go through them in a certain sequence — like first I have to reread the plan, then check the logs, then mentally count through the steps I think I verified. And if I lose my place, or if someone interrupts me, or if I suddenly think, “Wait, did I really confirm that part?” then I’ll often feel like I have to go back and start parts of it again. Not because I want to be extra careful in a normal way, but because it doesn’t feel safe unless I’ve followed the process correctly.\n\nIt happens with door locks too. I can’t just glance and move on. I’ll check, then kind of mentally mark it off, and sometimes count through what I did in my head. And old messages — I’ll reread them in this same repetitive way, like I’m looking for some phrase or warning I might have missed.\n\nThe frustrating thing is that the rule isn’t always logical. It’s more like a feeling of completeness. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, once you’ve gone through these steps, you can stop,” but then it doesn’t feel complete, so I keep going. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone else leaves because I couldn’t get that feeling that the checking was finished. If I try to stop before it feels safe, I panic, like I’ve left something unresolved that could come back badly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your obsessive thoughts and need to perform these rituals are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. I think that’s actually the part that scares me the most, because it’s not just something happening privately in my head anymore. It’s changing how I function, and how people treat me.\n\nThe biggest example is work. I missed a major cloud migration deadline because I just could not finish signing off on decisions. I kept reviewing, revisiting, wanting one more confirmation, one more pass, one more assurance that we weren’t making the wrong call. And it wasn’t like I didn’t understand the technical issues — I did. That’s my job. But I couldn’t get to the point of saying, “Okay, this is good enough, we can move forward.” I got stuck in that loop, and the deadline passed.\n\nAnd since then, I can tell my team has started working around me. They don’t say it in a cruel way, but I notice it. Decisions get made without waiting for me the way they used to. People bring me things later, after they’ve already narrowed the options, or they try to route approvals through someone else because they know I might hold it up. That feels awful, honestly. I’m supposed to be the person they rely on, and now I feel like I’ve become a bottleneck they have to manage.\n\nIt’s affecting my personal life too. I used to have routines that kept me sane — basketball, salsa nights, just being around people and not being inside work or inside my own head all the time. And I’ve stopped showing up. At first I’d tell myself I was just too busy, or I had something important to finish, but after a while I realized I was just disappearing from those parts of my life. I’m losing contact with people, and I’m losing the things that made me feel grounded.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering with my quality of life. It’s affecting my work, my reputation, my friendships, even the basic structure of my week. It feels like my life has gotten smaller around this need to be absolutely sure."
